People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I feel like I’m always going from one thing to the next. Like I can’t just sit and idle, if that makes sense. And with the new setup at work, it’s gotten harder to kind of cover that up.

I do customer service, so I’m used to having a rhythm, you know, calls and notes and all that, but lately it’s like I’m bouncing between things more noticeably. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything. I just feel like I’m constantly in motion, even when I’m supposed to be staying put.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.

And then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s more like my brain wandered off somewhere else. Like the other day they changed one of the steps for handling a certain call type, and I swear I read it, but then when I went to use it I thought, wait, what did that even say? So yeah, staying with one thing for a long stretch is hard.

I just feel like I’m constantly in motion, even when I’m supposed to be staying put.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, in a way. Like, I don’t go out doing wild things or anything, but at work I’ll hear someone nearby trying to work through a customer issue, and before they’re even done explaining it, I’ll just jump in with the answer.\n\nI’m not trying to take over or be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before I’ve really thought, “Hey, maybe let them finish,” or “Maybe nobody asked you.” Then afterward I’ll think, okay, that probably came off badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s more like my brain wandered off somewhere else. Like the other day they changed one of the steps for handling a certain call type, and I swear I read it, but then when I went to use it I thought, wait, what did that even say? So yeah, staying with one thing for a long stretch is hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially if I’m sitting through, like, a training video at work or even a slow appointment like this kind of thing—not that this is slow, I mean just appointments in general.\n\nI start feeling kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up or stretch or mess with something in my hands. Sometimes I’ll shift around a lot and then realize I’m probably moving more than other people are. It’s not horrible, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your behavior or impulsivity affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m trying to be difficult, but if I hear a coworker working through a customer problem, I’ll just sort of jump in with the answer before they’ve even finished explaining it. Or before anyone actually asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I can tell after the fact, like… okay, maybe I stepped on their toes there. I mean, in my head I’m helping, because I know how frustrating those calls can be, especially when the customer’s already been bounced around—actually we had one like that last week, and the whole system was lagging, which makes everybody tense—but yeah, the main thing is I interrupt. I don’t mean it rude, but I think it can come off that way with coworkers.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s more like my brain wandered off somewhere else. Like the other day they changed one of the steps for handling a certain call type, and I swear I read it, but then when I went to use it I thought, wait, what did that even say? So yeah, staying with one thing for a long stretch is hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially if I’m sitting through, like, a training video at work or even a slow appointment like this kind of thing—not that this is slow, I mean just appointments in general.\n\nI start feeling kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up or stretch or mess with something in my hands. Sometimes I’ll shift around a lot and then realize I’m probably moving more than other people are. It’s not horrible, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your behavior or impulsivity affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m trying to be difficult, but if I hear a coworker working through a customer problem, I’ll just sort of jump in with the answer before they’ve even finished explaining it. Or before anyone actually asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I can tell after the fact, like… okay, maybe I stepped on their toes there. I mean, in my head I’m helping, because I know how frustrating those calls can be, especially when the customer’s already been bounced around—actually we had one like that last week, and the whole system was lagging, which makes everybody tense—but yeah, the main thing is I interrupt. I don’t mean it rude, but I think it can come off that way with coworkers.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just this current job or getting older or whatever. In grade school I remember teachers would write things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn.\n\nMy mom used to save some of those report cards in this folder—actually, I think she still has a box of old school stuff somewhere, unless she tossed it when she moved—but yeah, the comments were always kind of the same. I’d have to be reminded to stay on my work. So, yeah, it feels like it’s been there a long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, mostly at work. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it’s showing up in my reviews now.

in grade school my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, talked out of turn, and had to be reminded to stay with my work.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has started showing up in my reviews, with supervisors pointing out unfinished tickets and mistakes that slow the team down even though I’m trying hard and generally get along well with customers.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about 1 year and 10 months, since my job shifted into the new hybrid chat and CRM role.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’d have to be reminded to stay on my work. And I usually do okay with customers, like I can talk to people and smooth things over—well, most of the time, depending on the customer, you know how some people call already mad because they’ve been transferred three times—but the paperwork or follow-through part, that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nSo yeah, it causes stress. I don’t like feeling like I’m working hard and still being told I’m holding things up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity, focus, and restlessness consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been pretty consistent. And with the new setup at work, it’s gotten harder to kind of cover that up.\n\nI do customer service, so I’m used to having a rhythm, you know, calls and notes and all that, but lately it’s like I’m bouncing between things more noticeably. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything. I just feel like I’m constantly in motion, even when I’m supposed to be staying put.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, in a way. Like, I don’t go out doing wild things or anything, but at work I’ll hear someone nearby trying to work through a customer issue, and before they’re even done explaining it, I’ll just jump in with the answer.\n\nI’m not trying to take over or be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before I’ve really thought, “Hey, maybe let them finish,” or “Maybe nobody asked you.” Then afterward I’ll think, okay, that probably came off badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it’s showing up in my reviews now.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or little mistakes that end up slowing the team down, which is frustrating because I really am trying. And I usually do okay with customers, like I can talk to people and smooth things over—well, most of the time, depending on the customer, you know how some people call already mad because they’ve been transferred three times—but the paperwork or follow-through part, that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nSo yeah, it causes stress. I don’t like feeling like I’m working hard and still being told I’m holding things up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity, focus, and restlessness consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been pretty consistent. Looking back, it was around way before this job or any recent change.\n\nIn grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work a lot. It’s funny, those old report card comments stick with you—my mom had a folder of them somewhere, and they all kind of sounded the same. So yeah, it feels like this has followed me from when I was a kid into now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing, like interrupting others or having trouble focusing, causing problems in your relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. My brother is worse about it than me, honestly—well, not worse, just loud. Holidays at our house are like everyone talking at once, so it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just how we are.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed major relationship problems from it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven’t lost a job or anything like that, no. And I wouldn’t say a relationship ended over it.

The main consequence is more at work, like it’s starting to show up in reviews. My supervisors have brought up unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow things down for everyone, even though I’m really trying. Which is embarrassing, because I usually do pretty well with customers—people will call in already upset, and I can usually talk them through it, depending on the day and the person—but then the follow-up part or the little details come back to bite me.

So not a huge blow-up consequence, I guess, but it’s becoming a real problem there.

Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s just like my mouth gets there before I’ve really thought, “Hey, maybe let them finish,” or “Maybe nobody asked you.” Then afterward I’ll think, okay, that probably came off badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. I don’t mean it rude, but I think it can come off that way with coworkers.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just this current job or getting older or whatever. In grade school I remember teachers would write things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn.\n\nMy mom used to save some of those report cards in this folder—actually, I think she still has a box of old school stuff somewhere, unless she tossed it when she moved—but yeah, the comments were always kind of the same. I’d have to be reminded to stay on my work. So, yeah, it feels like it’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or interrupting others are causing you significant problems or stress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, mostly at work. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it’s showing up in my reviews now.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or little mistakes that end up slowing the team down, which is frustrating because I really am trying. And I usually do okay with customers, like I can talk to people and smooth things over—well, most of the time, depending on the customer, you know how some people call already mad because they’ve been transferred three times—but the paperwork or follow-through part, that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nSo yeah, it causes stress. I don’t like feeling like I’m working hard and still being told I’m holding things up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity, focus, and restlessness consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been pretty consistent. Looking back, it was around way before this job or any recent change.\n\nIn grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work a lot. It’s funny, those old report card comments stick with you—my mom had a folder of them somewhere, and they all kind of sounded the same. So yeah, it feels like this has followed me from when I was a kid into now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing, like interrupting others or having trouble focusing, causing problems in your relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, outside of work… probably not in any big dramatic way that I can point to.\n\nLike, my family might say I get distracted or I cut in sometimes, but families say that kind of thing, you know? My brother is worse about it than me, honestly—well, not worse, just loud. Holidays at our house are like everyone talking at once, so it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just how we are.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed major relationship problems from it. Not like someone sitting me down and saying, “You need to fix this.” Mostly the stress I’m aware of is work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your impulsivity, inattention, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that, no. My supervisors have brought up unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow things down for everyone, even though I’m really trying. Which is embarrassing, because I usually do pretty well with customers—people will call in already upset, and I can usually talk them through it, depending on the day and the person—but then the follow-up part or the little details come back to bite me.\n\nSo not a huge blow-up consequence, I guess, but it’s becoming a real problem there.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing significant problems or stress in your personal life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant outside of work. I mean, I get stressed at home sometimes, but everybody does, right? Bills, family stuff, the house needing things fixed—my garage has been half-cleaned for, I don’t know, months, but that’s not really what you’re asking.\n\nNobody in my personal life has really said, like, “This is a major problem.” So I guess… not that I’ve noticed. Most of the real stress I can point to is still work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness happen more often when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I feel like I’m always going from one thing to the next. Like I can’t just sit and idle, if that makes sense. And with the new setup at work, it’s gotten harder to kind of cover that up.\n\nI do customer service, so I’m used to having a rhythm, you know, calls and notes and all that, but lately it’s like I’m bouncing between things more noticeably. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything. I just feel like I’m constantly in motion, even when I’m supposed to be staying put.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, in a way. Like, I don’t go out doing wild things or anything, but at work I’ll hear someone nearby trying to work through a customer issue, and before they’re even done explaining it, I’ll just jump in with the answer.\n\nI’m not trying to take over or be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before I’ve really thought, “Hey, maybe let them finish,” or “Maybe nobody asked you.” Then afterward I’ll think, okay, that probably came off badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s more like my brain wandered off somewhere else. Like the other day they changed one of the steps for handling a certain call type, and I swear I read it, but then when I went to use it I thought, wait, what did that even say? Especially if I’m sitting through, like, a training video at work or even a slow appointment like this kind of thing—not that this is slow, I mean just appointments in general.\n\nI start feeling kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up or stretch or mess with something in my hands. Sometimes I’ll shift around a lot and then realize I’m probably moving more than other people are. It’s not horrible, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your behavior or impulsivity affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m trying to be difficult, but if I hear a coworker working through a customer problem, I’ll just sort of jump in with the answer before they’ve even finished explaining it. Or before anyone actually asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I can tell after the fact, like… okay, maybe I stepped on their toes there. I mean, in my head I’m helping, because I know how frustrating those calls can be, especially when the customer’s already been bounced around—actually we had one like that last week, and the whole system was lagging, which makes everybody tense—but yeah, the main thing is I interrupt. I don’t mean it rude, but I think it can come off that way with coworkers.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just this current job or getting older or whatever. In grade school I remember teachers would write things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn.\n\nMy mom used to save some of those report cards in this folder—actually, I think she still has a box of old school stuff somewhere, unless she tossed it when she moved—but yeah, the comments were always kind of the same. I’d have to be reminded to stay on my work. So, yeah, it feels like it’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or interrupting others are causing you significant problems or stress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, mostly at work. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it’s showing up in my reviews now.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or little mistakes that end up slowing the team down, which is frustrating because I really am trying. And I usually do okay with customers, like I can talk to people and smooth things over—well, most of the time, depending on the customer, you know how some people call already mad because they’ve been transferred three times—but the paperwork or follow-through part, that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nSo yeah, it causes stress. I don’t like feeling like I’m working hard and still being told I’m holding things up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity, focus, and restlessness consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been pretty consistent. Looking back, it was around way before this job or any recent change.\n\nIn grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work a lot. It’s funny, those old report card comments stick with you—my mom had a folder of them somewhere, and they all kind of sounded the same. So yeah, it feels like this has followed me from when I was a kid into now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing, like interrupting others or having trouble focusing, causing problems in your relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, outside of work… probably not in any big dramatic way that I can point to.\n\nLike, my family might say I get distracted or I cut in sometimes, but families say that kind of thing, you know? My brother is worse about it than me, honestly—well, not worse, just loud. Holidays at our house are like everyone talking at once, so it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just how we are.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed major relationship problems from it. Not like someone sitting me down and saying, “You need to fix this.” Mostly the stress I’m aware of is work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your impulsivity, inattention, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that, no. And I wouldn’t say a relationship ended over it.\n\nThe main consequence is more at work, like it’s starting to show up in reviews. My supervisors have brought up unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow things down for everyone, even though I’m really trying. Which is embarrassing, because I usually do pretty well with customers—people will call in already upset, and I can usually talk them through it, depending on the day and the person—but then the follow-up part or the little details come back to bite me.\n\nSo not a huge blow-up consequence, I guess, but it’s becoming a real problem there.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing significant problems or stress in your personal life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant outside of work. I mean, I get stressed at home sometimes, but everybody does, right? Bills, family stuff, the house needing things fixed—my garage has been half-cleaned for, I don’t know, months, but that’s not really what you’re asking.\n\nNobody in my personal life has really said, like, “This is a major problem.” So I guess… not that I’ve noticed. Most of the real stress I can point to is still work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness happen more often when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. I think it shows up more when other people are talking through something, especially at work.\n\nLike if I hear a coworker trying to figure out a customer issue, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished explaining it, or before anyone even asked me. I don’t mean it in a rude way—I’m usually trying to help—but I can see how it probably comes off like I’m butting in.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but being around people definitely gives me more chances to do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness mostly happen in social situations or can they occur when you're alone as well?\n\nPatient: It can happen when I’m alone too, yeah. Like if I’m sitting through one of those training videos at home or at work, or even in a slow appointment, I start feeling kind of boxed in. Like I need to stand up, stretch, fiddle with something—just do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, some of those training videos are only, what, fifteen minutes, but they feel way longer. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just watch the thing,” and then I’m shifting around or looking for a reason to get up. Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to interfere, mostly at work. I was going to say “not daily,” because some days go fine, but then it shows up in my reviews.

Not like someone sitting me down and saying, “You need to fix this.” Mostly the stress I’m aware of is work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your impulsivity, inattention, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that, no. And I wouldn’t say a relationship ended over it.\n\nThe main consequence is more at work, like it’s starting to show up in reviews. My supervisors have brought up unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow things down for everyone, even though I’m really trying. Which is embarrassing, because I usually do pretty well with customers—people will call in already upset, and I can usually talk them through it, depending on the day and the person—but then the follow-up part or the little details come back to bite me.\n\nSo not a huge blow-up consequence, I guess, but it’s becoming a real problem there.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing significant problems or stress in your personal life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant outside of work. I mean, I get stressed at home sometimes, but everybody does, right? Bills, family stuff, the house needing things fixed—my garage has been half-cleaned for, I don’t know, months, but that’s not really what you’re asking.\n\nNobody in my personal life has really said, like, “This is a major problem.” So I guess… not that I’ve noticed. Most of the real stress I can point to is still work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness happen more often when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. I think it shows up more when other people are talking through something, especially at work.\n\nLike if I hear a coworker trying to figure out a customer issue, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished explaining it, or before anyone even asked me. I don’t mean it in a rude way—I’m usually trying to help—but I can see how it probably comes off like I’m butting in.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but being around people definitely gives me more chances to do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness mostly happen in social situations or can they occur when you're alone as well?\n\nPatient: It can happen when I’m alone too, yeah. Like if I’m sitting through one of those training videos at home or at work, or even in a slow appointment, I start feeling kind of boxed in. Like I need to stand up, stretch, fiddle with something—just do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, some of those training videos are only, what, fifteen minutes, but they feel way longer. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just watch the thing,” and then I’m shifting around or looking for a reason to get up. And with the new setup at work, it’s gotten harder to kind of cover that up.\n\nI do customer service, so I’m used to having a rhythm, you know, calls and notes and all that, but lately it’s like I’m bouncing between things more noticeably. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything. I just feel like I’m constantly in motion, even when I’m supposed to be staying put.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, in a way. Like, I don’t go out doing wild things or anything, but at work I’ll hear someone nearby trying to work through a customer issue, and before they’re even done explaining it, I’ll just jump in with the answer.\n\nI’m not trying to take over or be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before I’ve really thought, “Hey, maybe let them finish,” or “Maybe nobody asked you.” Then afterward I’ll think, okay, that probably came off badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s more like my brain wandered off somewhere else. Like the other day they changed one of the steps for handling a certain call type, and I swear I read it, but then when I went to use it I thought, wait, what did that even say? So yeah, staying with one thing for a long stretch is hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially if I’m sitting through, like, a training video at work or even a slow appointment like this kind of thing—not that this is slow, I mean just appointments in general.\n\nI start feeling kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up or stretch or mess with something in my hands. Sometimes I’ll shift around a lot and then realize I’m probably moving more than other people are. It’s not horrible, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your behavior or impulsivity affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m trying to be difficult, but if I hear a coworker working through a customer problem, I’ll just sort of jump in with the answer before they’ve even finished explaining it. Or before anyone actually asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I can tell after the fact, like… okay, maybe I stepped on their toes there. I mean, in my head I’m helping, because I know how frustrating those calls can be, especially when the customer’s already been bounced around—actually we had one like that last week, and the whole system was lagging, which makes everybody tense—but yeah, the main thing is I interrupt. I don’t mean it rude, but I think it can come off that way with coworkers.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just this current job or getting older or whatever. In grade school I remember teachers would write things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn.\n\nMy mom used to save some of those report cards in this folder—actually, I think she still has a box of old school stuff somewhere, unless she tossed it when she moved—but yeah, the comments were always kind of the same. I’d have to be reminded to stay on my work. So, yeah, it feels like it’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or interrupting others are causing you significant problems or stress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, mostly at work. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it’s showing up in my reviews now.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or little mistakes that end up slowing the team down, which is frustrating because I really am trying. And I usually do okay with customers, like I can talk to people and smooth things over—well, most of the time, depending on the customer, you know how some people call already mad because they’ve been transferred three times—but the paperwork or follow-through part, that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nSo yeah, it causes stress. I don’t like feeling like I’m working hard and still being told I’m holding things up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity, focus, and restlessness consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been pretty consistent. Looking back, it was around way before this job or any recent change.\n\nIn grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work a lot. It’s funny, those old report card comments stick with you—my mom had a folder of them somewhere, and they all kind of sounded the same. So yeah, it feels like this has followed me from when I was a kid into now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing, like interrupting others or having trouble focusing, causing problems in your relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, outside of work… probably not in any big dramatic way that I can point to.\n\nLike, my family might say I get distracted or I cut in sometimes, but families say that kind of thing, you know? My brother is worse about it than me, honestly—well, not worse, just loud. Holidays at our house are like everyone talking at once, so it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just how we are.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed major relationship problems from it. Not like someone sitting me down and saying, “You need to fix this.” Mostly the stress I’m aware of is work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your impulsivity, inattention, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that, no. And I wouldn’t say a relationship ended over it.\n\nThe main consequence is more at work, like it’s starting to show up in reviews. My supervisors have brought up unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow things down for everyone, even though I’m really trying. Which is embarrassing, because I usually do pretty well with customers—people will call in already upset, and I can usually talk them through it, depending on the day and the person—but then the follow-up part or the little details come back to bite me.\n\nSo not a huge blow-up consequence, I guess, but it’s becoming a real problem there.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing significant problems or stress in your personal life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant outside of work. I mean, I get stressed at home sometimes, but everybody does, right? Bills, family stuff, the house needing things fixed—my garage has been half-cleaned for, I don’t know, months, but that’s not really what you’re asking.\n\nNobody in my personal life has really said, like, “This is a major problem.” So I guess… not that I’ve noticed. Most of the real stress I can point to is still work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness happen more often when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. I think it shows up more when other people are talking through something, especially at work.\n\nLike if I hear a coworker trying to figure out a customer issue, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished explaining it, or before anyone even asked me. I don’t mean it in a rude way—I’m usually trying to help—but I can see how it probably comes off like I’m butting in.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but being around people definitely gives me more chances to do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness mostly happen in social situations or can they occur when you're alone as well?\n\nPatient: It can happen when I’m alone too, yeah. Like if I’m sitting through one of those training videos at home or at work, or even in a slow appointment, I start feeling kind of boxed in. Like I need to stand up, stretch, fiddle with something—just do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, some of those training videos are only, what, fifteen minutes, but they feel way longer. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just watch the thing,” and then I’m shifting around or looking for a reason to get up. So it’s not only social situations, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to interfere, mostly at work. I was going to say “not daily,” because some days go fine, but then it shows up in my reviews.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets and little mistakes that end up slowing the team down. And that bothers me because I’m trying hard, and I think I’m good with customers overall. I mean, I get along with people pretty well, and customers usually aren’t the issue. It’s more the follow-through stuff, the tickets sitting there, or something I missed that somebody else has to fix.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering some. Not ruining everything, but enough that it’s getting noticed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your symptoms are currently causing you more than minor problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… more than minor, at least at work. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because I still get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard. I’m not, you know, sitting there not caring.\n\nBut it’s showing up in my reviews now. Supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow everybody down, and that’s embarrassing. It’s like—sometimes one little thing I missed turns into somebody else having to clean it up, and I hate that. So yeah, I’d say it’s more than just a small annoyance.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah, I do feel overwhelmed sometimes, mostly when it comes back around at work. Like, I’ll think I’m doing okay, and then a review comes up and my supervisor points out tickets I didn’t finish or some mistake I made that slowed things down.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I feel like I’m always going from one thing to the next. Like I can’t just sit and idle, if that makes sense. And with the new setup at work, it’s gotten harder to kind of cover that up.\n\nI do customer service, so I’m used to having a rhythm, you know, calls and notes and all that, but lately it’s like I’m bouncing between things more noticeably. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything. I just feel like I’m constantly in motion, even when I’m supposed to be staying put.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, in a way. Like, I don’t go out doing wild things or anything, but at work I’ll hear someone nearby trying to work through a customer issue, and before they’re even done explaining it, I’ll just jump in with the answer.\n\nI’m not trying to take over or be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before I’ve really thought, “Hey, maybe let them finish,” or “Maybe nobody asked you.” Then afterward I’ll think, okay, that probably came off badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s more like my brain wandered off somewhere else. Like the other day they changed one of the steps for handling a certain call type, and I swear I read it, but then when I went to use it I thought, wait, what did that even say? Especially if I’m sitting through, like, a training video at work or even a slow appointment like this kind of thing—not that this is slow, I mean just appointments in general.\n\nI start feeling kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up or stretch or mess with something in my hands. Sometimes I’ll shift around a lot and then realize I’m probably moving more than other people are. It’s not horrible, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your behavior or impulsivity affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m trying to be difficult, but if I hear a coworker working through a customer problem, I’ll just sort of jump in with the answer before they’ve even finished explaining it. Or before anyone actually asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I can tell after the fact, like… okay, maybe I stepped on their toes there. I mean, in my head I’m helping, because I know how frustrating those calls can be, especially when the customer’s already been bounced around—actually we had one like that last week, and the whole system was lagging, which makes everybody tense—but yeah, the main thing is I interrupt. I don’t mean it rude, but I think it can come off that way with coworkers.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just this current job or getting older or whatever. In grade school I remember teachers would write things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn.\n\nMy mom used to save some of those report cards in this folder—actually, I think she still has a box of old school stuff somewhere, unless she tossed it when she moved—but yeah, the comments were always kind of the same. I’d have to be reminded to stay on my work. So, yeah, it feels like it’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or interrupting others are causing you significant problems or stress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, mostly at work. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it’s showing up in my reviews now.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or little mistakes that end up slowing the team down, which is frustrating because I really am trying. And I usually do okay with customers, like I can talk to people and smooth things over—well, most of the time, depending on the customer, you know how some people call already mad because they’ve been transferred three times—but the paperwork or follow-through part, that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nSo yeah, it causes stress. I don’t like feeling like I’m working hard and still being told I’m holding things up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity, focus, and restlessness consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been pretty consistent. Looking back, it was around way before this job or any recent change.\n\nIn grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work a lot. It’s funny, those old report card comments stick with you—my mom had a folder of them somewhere, and they all kind of sounded the same. So yeah, it feels like this has followed me from when I was a kid into now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing, like interrupting others or having trouble focusing, causing problems in your relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, outside of work… probably not in any big dramatic way that I can point to.\n\nLike, my family might say I get distracted or I cut in sometimes, but families say that kind of thing, you know? My brother is worse about it than me, honestly—well, not worse, just loud. Holidays at our house are like everyone talking at once, so it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just how we are.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed major relationship problems from it. Not like someone sitting me down and saying, “You need to fix this.” Mostly the stress I’m aware of is work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your impulsivity, inattention, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that, no. My supervisors have brought up unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow things down for everyone, even though I’m really trying. I mean, I get stressed at home sometimes, but everybody does, right? Bills, family stuff, the house needing things fixed—my garage has been half-cleaned for, I don’t know, months, but that’s not really what you’re asking.\n\nNobody in my personal life has really said, like, “This is a major problem.” So I guess… not that I’ve noticed. Most of the real stress I can point to is still work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness happen more often when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. I just feel like I’m constantly in motion, even when I’m supposed to be staying put.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, in a way. Like, I don’t go out doing wild things or anything, but at work I’ll hear someone nearby trying to work through a customer issue, and before they’re even done explaining it, I’ll just jump in with the answer.\n\nI’m not trying to take over or be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before I’ve really thought, “Hey, maybe let them finish,” or “Maybe nobody asked you.” Then afterward I’ll think, okay, that probably came off badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always at first, like if it’s something new I can kind of lock in for a bit, but in those longer coaching meetings, or if I’m trying to read through a new procedure at work, after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving across the page but I didn’t actually take in any of it.\n\nAnd then I have to go back, which is annoying because it’s not like the words are hard. It’s more like my brain wandered off somewhere else. Like the other day they changed one of the steps for handling a certain call type, and I swear I read it, but then when I went to use it I thought, wait, what did that even say? So yeah, staying with one thing for a long stretch is hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially if I’m sitting through, like, a training video at work or even a slow appointment like this kind of thing—not that this is slow, I mean just appointments in general.\n\nI start feeling kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up or stretch or mess with something in my hands. Sometimes I’ll shift around a lot and then realize I’m probably moving more than other people are. It’s not horrible, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your behavior or impulsivity affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m trying to be difficult, but if I hear a coworker working through a customer problem, I’ll just sort of jump in with the answer before they’ve even finished explaining it. Or before anyone actually asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I can tell after the fact, like… okay, maybe I stepped on their toes there. I mean, in my head I’m helping, because I know how frustrating those calls can be, especially when the customer’s already been bounced around—actually we had one like that last week, and the whole system was lagging, which makes everybody tense—but yeah, the main thing is I interrupt. I don’t mean it rude, but I think it can come off that way with coworkers.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just this current job or getting older or whatever. In grade school I remember teachers would write things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn.\n\nMy mom used to save some of those report cards in this folder—actually, I think she still has a box of old school stuff somewhere, unless she tossed it when she moved—but yeah, the comments were always kind of the same. I’d have to be reminded to stay on my work. So, yeah, it feels like it’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or interrupting others are causing you significant problems or stress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, mostly at work. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it’s showing up in my reviews now.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or little mistakes that end up slowing the team down, which is frustrating because I really am trying. And I usually do okay with customers, like I can talk to people and smooth things over—well, most of the time, depending on the customer, you know how some people call already mad because they’ve been transferred three times—but the paperwork or follow-through part, that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nSo yeah, it causes stress. I don’t like feeling like I’m working hard and still being told I’m holding things up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity, focus, and restlessness consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been pretty consistent. Looking back, it was around way before this job or any recent change.\n\nIn grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work a lot. It’s funny, those old report card comments stick with you—my mom had a folder of them somewhere, and they all kind of sounded the same. So yeah, it feels like this has followed me from when I was a kid into now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing, like interrupting others or having trouble focusing, causing problems in your relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, outside of work… probably not in any big dramatic way that I can point to.\n\nLike, my family might say I get distracted or I cut in sometimes, but families say that kind of thing, you know? My brother is worse about it than me, honestly—well, not worse, just loud. Holidays at our house are like everyone talking at once, so it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just how we are.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed major relationship problems from it. Not like someone sitting me down and saying, “You need to fix this.” Mostly the stress I’m aware of is work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your impulsivity, inattention, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that, no. And I wouldn’t say a relationship ended over it.\n\nThe main consequence is more at work, like it’s starting to show up in reviews. My supervisors have brought up unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow things down for everyone, even though I’m really trying. Which is embarrassing, because I usually do pretty well with customers—people will call in already upset, and I can usually talk them through it, depending on the day and the person—but then the follow-up part or the little details come back to bite me.\n\nSo not a huge blow-up consequence, I guess, but it’s becoming a real problem there.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing significant problems or stress in your personal life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant outside of work. I mean, I get stressed at home sometimes, but everybody does, right? Bills, family stuff, the house needing things fixed—my garage has been half-cleaned for, I don’t know, months, but that’s not really what you’re asking.\n\nNobody in my personal life has really said, like, “This is a major problem.” So I guess… not that I’ve noticed. Most of the real stress I can point to is still work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness happen more often when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. I think it shows up more when other people are talking through something, especially at work.\n\nLike if I hear a coworker trying to figure out a customer issue, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished explaining it, or before anyone even asked me. I don’t mean it in a rude way—I’m usually trying to help—but I can see how it probably comes off like I’m butting in.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but being around people definitely gives me more chances to do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness mostly happen in social situations or can they occur when you're alone as well?\n\nPatient: It can happen when I’m alone too, yeah. Like if I’m sitting through one of those training videos at home or at work, or even in a slow appointment, I start feeling kind of boxed in. Like I need to stand up, stretch, fiddle with something—just do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, some of those training videos are only, what, fifteen minutes, but they feel way longer. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just watch the thing,” and then I’m shifting around or looking for a reason to get up. So it’s not only social situations, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to interfere, mostly at work. I was going to say “not daily,” because some days go fine, but then it shows up in my reviews.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets and little mistakes that end up slowing the team down. And that bothers me because I’m trying hard, and I think I’m good with customers overall. I mean, I get along with people pretty well, and customers usually aren’t the issue. It’s more the follow-through stuff, the tickets sitting there, or something I missed that somebody else has to fix.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering some. Not ruining everything, but enough that it’s getting noticed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your symptoms are currently causing you more than minor problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… more than minor, at least at work. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because I still get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard. I’m not, you know, sitting there not caring.\n\nBut it’s showing up in my reviews now. Supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow everybody down, and that’s embarrassing. It’s like—sometimes one little thing I missed turns into somebody else having to clean it up, and I hate that. So yeah, I’d say it’s more than just a small annoyance.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by the symptoms you're experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do feel overwhelmed sometimes, mostly when it comes back around at work. Like, I’ll think I’m doing okay, and then a review comes up and my supervisor points out tickets I didn’t finish or some mistake I made that slowed things down.\n\nAnd that kind of sticks with me, because I really am trying. I usually do fine with customers—honestly, talking to people is not the worst part for me—but then the paperwork or tickets, whatever you want to call it, that’s where it shows. It’s frustrating more than anything. I don’t know if I’d say I’m in crisis over it, but yeah, it does get to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your symptoms as causing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s mostly occupational, at work. I don’t think I’m trying to be difficult, but if I hear a coworker working through a customer problem, I’ll just sort of jump in with the answer before they’ve even finished explaining it. Or before anyone actually asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I can tell after the fact, like… okay, maybe I stepped on their toes there. I mean, in my head I’m helping, because I know how frustrating those calls can be, especially when the customer’s already been bounced around—actually we had one like that last week, and the whole system was lagging, which makes everybody tense—but yeah, the main thing is I interrupt. I don’t mean it rude, but I think it can come off that way with coworkers.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just this current job or getting older or whatever. In grade school I remember teachers would write things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn.\n\nMy mom used to save some of those report cards in this folder—actually, I think she still has a box of old school stuff somewhere, unless she tossed it when she moved—but yeah, the comments were always kind of the same. I’d have to be reminded to stay on my work. So, yeah, it feels like it’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or interrupting others are causing you significant problems or stress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, mostly at work. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it’s showing up in my reviews now.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or little mistakes that end up slowing the team down, which is frustrating because I really am trying. And I usually do okay with customers, like I can talk to people and smooth things over—well, most of the time, depending on the customer, you know how some people call already mad because they’ve been transferred three times—but the paperwork or follow-through part, that’s where it catches up with me.\n\nSo yeah, it causes stress. I don’t like feeling like I’m working hard and still being told I’m holding things up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity, focus, and restlessness consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been pretty consistent. Looking back, it was around way before this job or any recent change.\n\nIn grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work a lot. It’s funny, those old report card comments stick with you—my mom had a folder of them somewhere, and they all kind of sounded the same. So yeah, it feels like this has followed me from when I was a kid into now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing, like interrupting others or having trouble focusing, causing problems in your relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, outside of work… probably not in any big dramatic way that I can point to.\n\nLike, my family might say I get distracted or I cut in sometimes, but families say that kind of thing, you know? My brother is worse about it than me, honestly—well, not worse, just loud. Holidays at our house are like everyone talking at once, so it’s hard to tell what’s me and what’s just how we are.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed major relationship problems from it. Not like someone sitting me down and saying, “You need to fix this.” Mostly the stress I’m aware of is work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your impulsivity, inattention, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job or anything like that, no. And I wouldn’t say a relationship ended over it.\n\nThe main consequence is more at work, like it’s starting to show up in reviews. My supervisors have brought up unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow things down for everyone, even though I’m really trying. Which is embarrassing, because I usually do pretty well with customers—people will call in already upset, and I can usually talk them through it, depending on the day and the person—but then the follow-up part or the little details come back to bite me.\n\nSo not a huge blow-up consequence, I guess, but it’s becoming a real problem there.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing significant problems or stress in your personal life, outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant outside of work. I mean, I get stressed at home sometimes, but everybody does, right? Bills, family stuff, the house needing things fixed—my garage has been half-cleaned for, I don’t know, months, but that’s not really what you’re asking.\n\nNobody in my personal life has really said, like, “This is a major problem.” So I guess… not that I’ve noticed. Most of the real stress I can point to is still work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness happen more often when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. I think it shows up more when other people are talking through something, especially at work.\n\nLike if I hear a coworker trying to figure out a customer issue, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished explaining it, or before anyone even asked me. I don’t mean it in a rude way—I’m usually trying to help—but I can see how it probably comes off like I’m butting in.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but being around people definitely gives me more chances to do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and restlessness mostly happen in social situations or can they occur when you're alone as well?\n\nPatient: It can happen when I’m alone too, yeah. Like if I’m sitting through one of those training videos at home or at work, or even in a slow appointment, I start feeling kind of boxed in. Like I need to stand up, stretch, fiddle with something—just do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s funny, some of those training videos are only, what, fifteen minutes, but they feel way longer. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just watch the thing,” and then I’m shifting around or looking for a reason to get up. So it’s not only social situations, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to interfere, mostly at work. I was going to say “not daily,” because some days go fine, but then it shows up in my reviews.\n\nMy supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets and little mistakes that end up slowing the team down. And that bothers me because I’m trying hard, and I think I’m good with customers overall. I mean, I get along with people pretty well, and customers usually aren’t the issue. It’s more the follow-through stuff, the tickets sitting there, or something I missed that somebody else has to fix.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering some. Not ruining everything, but enough that it’s getting noticed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your symptoms are currently causing you more than minor problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… more than minor, at least at work. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because I still get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard. I’m not, you know, sitting there not caring.\n\nBut it’s showing up in my reviews now. Supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets or mistakes that slow everybody down, and that’s embarrassing. It’s like—sometimes one little thing I missed turns into somebody else having to clean it up, and I hate that. So yeah, I’d say it’s more than just a small annoyance.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by the symptoms you're experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do feel overwhelmed sometimes, mostly when it comes back around at work. Like, I’ll think I’m doing okay, and then a review comes up and my supervisor points out tickets I didn’t finish or some mistake I made that slowed things down.\n\nAnd that kind of sticks with me, because I really am trying. I usually do fine with customers—honestly, talking to people is not the worst part for me—but then the paperwork or tickets, whatever you want to call it, that’s where it shows. It’s frustrating more than anything. I don’t know if I’d say I’m in crisis over it, but yeah, it does get to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your symptoms as causing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s mostly occupational, at work. I mean, socially I usually do okay—customers, for the most part, I get along with them fine. I used to work with this one guy who could make any customer mad in two minutes, and I’m kind of the opposite, I can usually smooth things over… but that’s not really what you asked.\n\nThe problem is the follow-through. It’s showing up in my reviews now, where supervisors are pointing out unfinished tickets or mistakes I made that slow the team down. And I’m trying hard, so it’s frustrating. It’s not like I don’t care. But yeah, when other people have to wait on me or fix something I missed, that feels pretty bad. So I’d say it is causing some real impairment at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly impacting your ability to function at work or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is, mainly at work. I mean, I can still talk to customers fine—actually that part’s usually okay. I’ve always been pretty good with people, like I can calm somebody down if they come in upset, which is funny because my brother used to say I should’ve been in sales, but anyway…\n\nThe part that’s causing problems is the stuff after that. Tickets not getting finished, or little mistakes I miss that end up slowing the team down. And it’s showing up in my reviews now, so it’s not just me being hard on myself. My supervisors have brought it up.\n\nSo yeah, I’m trying hard, but it’s definitely affecting how I function at work. It’s showing up in my reviews now, where supervisors are pointing out unfinished tickets or mistakes I made that slow the team down. And I’m trying hard, so it’s frustrating. It’s not like I don’t care. But yeah, when other people have to wait on me or fix something I missed, that feels pretty bad. So I’d say it is causing some real impairment at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly impacting your ability to function at work or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is, mainly at work. I mean, I can still talk to customers fine—actually that part’s usually okay. I’ve always been pretty good with people, like I can calm somebody down if they come in upset, which is funny because my brother used to say I should’ve been in sales, but anyway…\n\nThe part that’s causing problems is the stuff after that. Tickets not getting finished, or little mistakes I miss that end up slowing the team down. And it’s showing up in my reviews now, so it’s not just me being hard on myself. My supervisors have brought it up.\n\nSo yeah, I’m trying hard, but it’s definitely affecting how I function at work. Not so much in every area of life, but work for sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your symptoms are severe enough to be causing significant problems in your daily life most of the time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d put it that strong. It’s not like every day is falling apart. I still get through the day, and with customers I usually do pretty well. I mean, some people come in already mad, and I can usually talk them down—though there was this one regular who used to complain about everything, even the parking lot, which wasn’t even our department…

But, yeah, the work side is where it’s becoming a real problem. It’s showing up in my reviews now. Supervisors are pointing out unfinished tickets or mistakes I made that slow the team down, and that bothers me because I am trying. So I’d say it causes significant problems at work, but maybe not severe across my whole daily life most of the time.
